**Kendrick Lamar Duckworth** (born June 17, 1987) is an American rapper and songwriter. Rooted in [West Coast hip-hop](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Coast_hip-hop), his music features [conscious](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conscious_rap), introspective lyrics, with [political criticism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_criticism) and [social commentary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_commentary) concerning [African-American culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African-American_culture). [Music journalists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Music_journalists) have listed Lamar among the greatest rappers of all time. In 2018, he became the first musician outside of the [classical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Classical_music) and [jazz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jazz) genres to receive the [Pulitzer Prize for Music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pulitzer_Prize_for_Music).

Born and raised in [Compton, California](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Compton,_California), Lamar began releasing music under the stage name **K.Dot** in [high school](554992.md). He signed with [Top Dawg Entertainment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Top_Dawg_Entertainment) (TDE) in 2005 and co-founded the hip hop [supergroup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supergroup_\(music\)) [Black Hippy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_Hippy). His [alternative rap](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alternative_hip-hop) debut album, *[Section.80](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Section.80)*, led to a joint contract with [Dr. Dre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dr._Dre)'s [Aftermath Entertainment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aftermath_Entertainment) and [Interscope Records](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interscope_Records). He rose to stardom with his [gangsta rap](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gangsta_rap)–influenced second album *[Good Kid, M.A.A.D City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Good_Kid,_M.A.A.D_City)* (2012), which became the [longest-charting hip hop studio album](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Billboard_200#Most_weeks_on_the_chart) on the [*Billboard* 200](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Billboard_200) and was named the greatest [concept album](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Concept_album) of all time by *[Rolling Stone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rolling_Stone)*. In 2015, he had his first [*Billboard* Hot 100](423161.md) number-one single, with the remix of [Taylor Swift](5422144.md)'s "[Bad Blood](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bad_Blood_\(Taylor_Swift_song\))", and released his first of five consecutive number-one albums on the *Billboard* 200, *[To Pimp a Butterfly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/To_Pimp_a_Butterfly)*, which infused hip-hop with historical [African-American music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African-American_music) genres such as jazz, [funk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Funk), and [soul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soul_music).

Lamar's critical and commercial success continued with his [R\&B](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Contemporary_R\&B) and [pop](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pop_music)-leaning fourth album *[Damn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Damn_\(album\))* (2017), featuring his second US number-one single, "[Humble](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Humble_\(song\))". He contributed to [the soundtrack](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_Panther:_The_Album) of the 2018 film *[Black Panther](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_Panther_\(film\))*, earning a nomination for the [Academy Award for Best Original Song](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Academy_Award_for_Best_Original_Song) for "[All the Stars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/All_the_Stars)". His fifth album, *[Mr. Morale & the Big Steppers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mr._Morale_&_the_Big_Steppers)* (2022), delved into introspection and concluded his tenure with TDE and Aftermath. In 2024, his [highly publicized feud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Drake%E2%80%93Kendrick_Lamar_feud) with [Drake](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Drake_\(musician\)) and sixth album *[GNX](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/GNX_\(album\))* spawned the *Billboard* Hot 100 number-one singles "[Like That](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Like_That_\(Future,_Metro_Boomin_and_Kendrick_Lamar_song\))", "[Not Like Us](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Not_Like_Us)", "[Squabble Up](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Squabble_Up)", and "[Luther](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luther_\(Kendrick_Lamar_song\))". "Not Like Us" won five [Grammy Awards](44635.md), including [Song of the Year](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grammy_Award_for_Song_of_the_Year) and [Record of the Year](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grammy_Award_for_Record_of_the_Year), while "Luther" also won the latter and became his longest-charting number-one song in the US, at 13 weeks.

[Lamar's accolades](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_awards_and_nominations_received_by_Kendrick_Lamar) include 27 Grammy Awards—the [most for a rapper](16116273.md#Most_Grammys_won_by_a_rapper)—2 [Primetime Emmy Awards](6579025.md), a [Brit Award](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brit_Awards), 5 [American Music Awards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Music_Awards), 7 [*Billboard* Music Awards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Billboard_Music_Awards), 11 [MTV Video Music Awards](295217.md) and a record 37 [BET Hip Hop Awards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BET_Hip_Hop_Awards). *[Time](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Time_\(magazine\))* listed him as one of the [100 most influential people in the world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Time_100) in 2016. Three of his works were included in *[Rolling Stone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rolling_Stone)*'s 2020 revision of the "[500 Greatest Albums of All Time](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rolling_Stone's_500_Greatest_Albums_of_All_Time)". In 2025, he headlined the most-watched [Super Bowl halftime show](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Super_Bowl_LIX_halftime_show) in history, while his [Grand National Tour](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grand_National_Tour) with [SZA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SZA) became the highest-grossing co-headlining tour of all time. Outside of music, Lamar co-founded the creative company [PGLang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PGLang) and ventured into film with his longtime creative partner, [Dave Free](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dave_Free).

## Early life and education

Kendrick Lamar Duckworth was born on June 17, 1987, in Compton, California.[^cite_note-4] He is the first child of former [gangster](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gangster) Kenneth "Kenny" Duckworth and hairdresser Paula Oliver. Both of his parents are [African-Americans](2154.md) from [South Side, Chicago](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Side,_Chicago).[^cite_note-Eells-5] When they were teenagers, they relocated to Compton in 1984, due to his father's affiliation with the [Gangster Disciples](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gangster_Disciples).[^cite_note-Edwards-6] Lamar was named after singer-songwriter [Eddie Kendricks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eddie_Kendricks) of [the Temptations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Temptations).[^cite_note-7] He was described as a "loner" by his mother.[^cite_note-Eells-5][^cite_note-Hopper-8] Eventually, his parents had his two younger brothers and younger sister, businesswoman Kayla Sawyer ([née](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Birth_name) Duckworth).[^cite_note-9] His cousins are former basketball player [Nick Young](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nick_Young_\(basketball\)) and rapper [Baby Keem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baby_Keem).[^cite_note-10]

Lamar and his family lived in [Section 8](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Section_8_\(housing\)) housing, were reliant on [welfare](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Welfare_spending) and [food stamps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supplemental_Nutrition_Assistance_Program), and experienced [homelessness](19394651.md).[^cite_note-11][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEThomas201951–66-12] Although he is not a member of a particular gang, he grew up with close affiliates of the [Westside Pirus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pirus).[^cite_note-Edwards-6] Despite suffering hardships, Lamar remembered having "good memories" of his childhood that sparked his interest in [hip hop music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hip_hop_music), such as sneaking into his parents' [house parties](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/House_party).[^cite_note-Eells-5][^cite_note-Greenburg-13] He felt "spiritually unsatisfied" as a child when attending [sermons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sermon), finding them empty and "one-sided" before discovering "more truth", adding "Our God is a loving God".[^cite_note-14]

After hearing a recording of his voice for the first time, Lamar became interested in [rapping](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rapping).[^cite_note-15] He was introduced to [police brutality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Police_brutality) after experiencing the first day of the [1992 Los Angeles riots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1992_Los_Angeles_riots).[^cite_note-Eells-5] When he was five years old, Lamar witnessed a murder for the first time while sitting outside of his apartment unit, as a teenage drug dealer was killed in a [drive-by shooting](65683.md).[^cite_note-Eells-5] "It done something to me right then and there," Lamar later admitted to [NPR Music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NPR_Music). "It let me know that this is not only something that I'm looking at, but it's something that maybe I have to get used to."[^cite_note-NPR_2015-16] His parents nicknamed him "Man-Man" due to his precocious behavior, although he confessed it "put a stigma on the idea of me reacting as a kid sometimes—I would hurt myself and they would expect me not to cry."[^cite_note-Hiatt-17]

In school, Lamar was a quiet and observant student who excelled academically and had a noticeable [stutter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stuttering).[^cite_note-18] His [first grade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_grade) teacher at Ronald E. McNair Elementary School encouraged him to become a writer after she heard him correctly use the word "audacity".[^cite_note-19][^cite_note-Moore-20] As a [seventh grade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seventh_grade) student at Vanguard Learning Center, Lamar was introduced to [poetry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Poetry) by his English teacher, Regis Inge.[^cite_note-Moore-20] Inge integrated the literary form into his curriculum as a response to the growing racial tensions amongst his students. Through its connection to hip hop, Lamar studied rhymes, [metaphors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metaphor) and [double entendres](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Double_entendres), which made him fall in love with songwriting: "You can put all your feelings down on a sheet of paper, and they'd make sense to you. I liked that."[^cite_note-Eells-5][^cite_note-Moore-20] Instead of completing assignments for other classes, Lamar would scribe lyrics in his notebooks.[^cite_note-Moore-20] His initial writing was entirely [profane](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Profanity), and helped him manage the [psychological trauma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Psychological_trauma) and [depression](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Depression_\(mood\)) he struggled with during his adolescence.[^cite_note-Moore-20][^cite_note-21] Inge played a vital role in his intellectual growth, often criticizing his [lexicon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lexicon) and suggesting prompts to strengthen his prose.[^cite_note-Moore-20]

Lamar later attended [Centennial High School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Centennial_High_School_\(Compton,_California\)).[^cite_note-Harling-22] He was enrolled in [summer school](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Summer_school) during the tenth grade, which he dreaded because it forced him to be embroiled in a [gang war](80408.md#Violence).[^cite_note-Harling-22] Despite his efforts to avoid them, Lamar soon became heavily involved with Compton's [hedonistic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hedonism) gang culture, which led to numerous health scares and encounters with the police.[^cite_note-Eells-5] He distanced himself from the lifestyle following an intervention staged by his father.[^cite_note-Hopper-8] When he was 16, Lamar was [baptized](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baptism) and [converted](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conversion_to_Christianity) to [Christianity](5211.md) following the death of a friend.[^cite_note-23][^cite_note-24] He graduated from high school in 2005 as a [straight-A](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Academic_grading_in_the_United_States) student.[^cite_note-25][^cite_note-Watson-26] He considered studying [psychology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Psychology) and [astronomy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Astronomy) in college, but suspended his academic pursuits to focus on his music career.[^cite_note-Eells-5][^cite_note-27]

## Career

### 2003–2008: Beginnings



Lamar began his career while he attended [Centennial High School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Centennial_High_School_\(Compton,_California\)).

During high school, Lamar adopted the [stage name](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stage_name) K.Dot and began [freestyling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freestyle_rap) and [battle rapping](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_rap) at school.[^cite_note-Jackson-28] His performances caught the attention of fellow student [Dave Free](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dave_Free), who traveled from [Inglewood](107640.md) to watch him rap.[^cite_note-Jackson-28] They quickly formed a friendship over their love of hip-hop and the television sitcom *[Martin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Martin_\(TV_series\))*.[^cite_note-Jackson-28] They recorded music together at Free's makeshift garage studio and at his older brother's [Hyde Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hyde_Park,_Los_Angeles) apartment.[^cite_note-Jackson-28] Lamar's earliest performances were held at a "super hood" [comedy club](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Comedy_club) and behind a tattoo parlor.[^cite_note-Jackson-28] Free was his [hype man](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hype_man) during that time, while his older brother was his manager and disc jockey.[^cite_note-Jackson-28] Lamar recorded five [mixtapes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mixtape) throughout the [2000s](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2000s_in_music); his first, *Youngest Head Nigga in Charge (Hub City Threat: Minor of the Year)*, was released in 2004 or 2005, through Konkrete Jungle Musik.[^cite_note-Mixtapes-29] The mixtapes primarily consisted of freestyles over the [production of popular hip-hop songs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hip-hop_production).[^cite_note-Mixtapes-29]

In a series of [retrospective](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Retrospective) reviews for *[Rolling Stone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rolling_Stone)*, Mosi Reeves complimented Lamar's "unerring" sense of rhythm and timing found in *Hub City Threat: Minor of the Year*, but criticized his "clumsy" lyricism and that his flow was "overly beholden to ... Jay-Z and Lil Wayne".[^cite_note-Mixtapes-29] Free, who was working as a [computer technician](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Computer_repair_technician), introduced the mixtape to record producer Anthony "Top Dawg" Tiffith while attempting to repair his computer.[^cite_note-Greenburg16-30] Tiffith was impressed with Lamar's burgeoning abilities and invited him to partake in an [audition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Audition) process for entry into his newly established [independent record label](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Independent_record_label), [Top Dawg Entertainment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Top_Dawg_Entertainment) (TDE).[^cite_note-Greenburg16-30] During his audition, Lamar freestyled for Tiffith and record executive [Terrence "Punch" Henderson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Punch_\(rapper\)) for two hours, a strategy that impressed Henderson but bewildered Tiffith.[^cite_note-Hopper-8][^cite_note-Billboard17-31] He was offered a [recording contract](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Recording_contract) by TDE in 2005, joining [Jay Rock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jay_Rock) as the label's first signings.[^cite_note-Hopper-8] Upon signing, he purchased a [minority stake](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minority_interest) in the label for an undisclosed amount.[^cite_note-Billboard17-31]

Lamar had a brief stint as a [security guard](19283898.md) when he started working on music with Jay Rock at TDE's in-house recording studio.[^cite_note-32][^cite_note-Watson-26] The bond he formed with him, [Ab-Soul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ab-Soul) and [Schoolboy Q](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Schoolboy_Q) led to the formation of the hip-hop [supergroup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supergroup_\(music\)), [Black Hippy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_Hippy).[^cite_note-33] Lamar released his second mixtape, *Training Day*, in 2007. Reeves complemented its varied production and "well-executed" concept, which was based on [the 2001 film](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Training_Day).[^cite_note-Mixtapes-29] In 2006, Lamar signed an [artist development deal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Artist_development_deal) with [Def Jam Recordings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Def_Jam_Recordings) and was featured on two [singles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Single_\(music\)) by [the Game](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Game_\(rapper\)). He also heavily contributed to Jay Rock's first two mixtapes, *Watts Finest Vol. 1* and *Watts Finest Vol. 2: The Nickerson Files*.[^cite_note-Hiatt-17][^cite_note-34] Lamar was ultimately let go from Def Jam after an encounter with its president and chief executive officer, [Jay-Z](162870.md); he later described it as "one of those situations where I wasn't ready."[^cite_note-35][^cite_note-36] Lamar and Jay Rock released a collaborative mixtape, titled *No Sleep 'til NYC*, on December 24, 2007. Reeves thought the project was a "fun cypher session, nothing more, nothing less."[^cite_note-Mixtapes-29]

### 2009–2011: *Overly Dedicated* and *Section.80*

Lamar's third mixtape *[C4](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/C4_\(mixtape\))*, released on January 30, 2009, is a [tribute project](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Album#Tribute_or_cover) to [Lil Wayne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lil_Wayne)'s *[Tha Carter III](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tha_Carter_III)* (2008) and was supported by his co-sign.[^cite_note-37] Reeves felt that the mixtape was a "wrongheaded homage to a year-old, well-worn album."[^cite_note-Mixtapes-29] From February to July, he toured with the Game on his [LAX Tour](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LAX_\(album\)#Tour) as a hype man for Jay Rock.[^cite_note-38][^cite_note-39] Lamar disliked how his stage name diverted attention away from his true identity, and decided to retire it.[^cite_note-40] He opted to use his first and middle names professionally and regards the name change as part of his career growth.[^cite_note-41] For his [eponymous debut extended play](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kendrick_Lamar_\(EP\)) (2009),[^cite_note-dopeboyz1-42] Lamar eschewed the creative process of his mixtapes in favor of a project heavily focused on his songwriting over "lovely yet doleful" production. Reeves described the EP as the "first standout project" of his career, praising its [melancholic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Melancholic) tone. He felt that the project restored his reputation following the sting of criticism he received over *C4*.[^cite_note-Mixtapes-29]



Lamar performing at [Sound Academy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rebel_\(entertainment_complex\)) in 2011, prior to the release of *[Section.80](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Section.80)*

After striking a [music publishing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Music_publishing) deal with [Warner/Chappell Music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Warner/Chappell_Music),[^cite_note-\:0-43] Lamar released his fourth mixtape, *[Overly Dedicated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Overly_Dedicated)*, on September 14, 2010. It was his first project to be purchased through digital retailers.[^cite_note-44] Reeves described *Overly Dedicated* as a partial "victory lap" that marked a shift in his songwriting.[^cite_note-Mixtapes-29] The mixtape peaked at number 72 on *[Billboard](18309966.md)*'s [Top R\&B/Hip-Hop Albums](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Top_R\&B/Hip-Hop_Albums) chart.[^cite_note-45] Lamar served as Jay Rock's hype man for a second time during [Tech N9ne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tech_N9ne)'s Independent Grind Tour, where *Overly Dedicated* was introduced to [Dr. Dre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dr._Dre).[^cite_note-hiphopdx1-46][^cite_note-47] After watching the music video for the song "Ignorance Is Bliss" on [YouTube](3524766.md), he reached out to Lamar with hopes of working with him and [Snoop Dogg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Snoop_Dogg) on his unfinished album, *Detox*.[^cite_note-hiphopdx1-46][^cite_note-48] He also considered signing him to his record label, [Aftermath Entertainment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aftermath_Entertainment), and was encouraged to by artists such as [J. Cole](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/J._Cole).[^cite_note-49][^cite_note-50]

Lamar entered a brief relationship with [Nitty Scott](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nitty_Scott),[^cite_note-51][^cite_note-52] and was featured on *[XXL](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/XXL_\(magazine\))*'s 2011 Freshman Class list.[^cite_note-53] He released his debut album, *[Section.80](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Section.80)*, on July 2, 2011,[^cite_note-54] which was supported by its lead single "[HiiiPower](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/HiiiPower)".[^cite_note-55] The album explored [conscious](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conscious_hip-hop) and [alternative hip-hop](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alternative_hip-hop) styles and experimented with "stripped-down" [jazz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jazz) production.[^cite_note-Breihan-2021-56][^cite_note-57] Ogden Payne of *[Forbes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forbes)* considers it to be "the genesis to \[Lamar] successfully balancing [social commentary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_commentary) with mass appeal."[^cite_note-58] *Section.80* marked Lamar's first appearance on the [*Billboard* 200](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Billboard_200) chart, where it peaked at number 113. It sold approximately 5,000 copies in its first week of tracking, with minimal coverage from [mainstream media](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mainstream_media) outlets.[^cite_note-59] To promote the album, Lamar performed at small venues and college campuses across the U.S.[^cite_note-60][^cite_note-61] He was dubbed the "[New King of the West Coast](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Honorific_nicknames_in_popular_music)" by Snoop Dogg, Dr. Dre and the Game during a performance in [West Los Angeles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Los_Angeles).[^cite_note-62][^cite_note-63] Throughout the year, he appeared on the Game's *[The R.E.D. Album](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_R.E.D._Album)*, Tech N9ne's *[All 6's and 7's](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/All_6's_and_7's)*, [9th Wonder](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/9th_Wonder)'s *[The Wonder Years](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Wonder_Years_\(9th_Wonder_album\))*, and [Drake](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Drake_\(musician\))'s *[Take Care](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Take_Care_\(album\))*.[^cite_note-64]

### 2012–2013: *Good Kid, M.A.A.D City*



Lamar performing at the [Pitchfork Music Festival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pitchfork_Music_Festival) in 2012

Lamar began planning a new album before *Section.80* was released.[^cite_note-65] From February to April 2012, he opened for Drake on his [Club Paradise Tour](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Club_Paradise_Tour).[^cite_note-66] He began working with J. Cole on a collaborative album around that time.[^cite_note-67][^cite_note-68] On February 14, Lamar would release the song "[Cartoon & Cereal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cartoon_and_Cereal)" for digital download, a track that features [Gunplay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gunplay_\(rapper\)).[^cite_note-69] On March 8, *[The Fader](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Fader)* reported that Lamar had signed a [joint venture](538782.md) recording contract with Aftermath Entertainment and [Interscope Records](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interscope_Records); under the deal, TDE continued to serve as his primary label.[^cite_note-MTV-70] His next single, "[The Recipe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Recipe_\(Kendrick_Lamar_song\))" featuring Dr. Dre, premiered on [rhythmic crossover](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rhythmic_crossover) radio on April 2.[^cite_note-71]

*[Good Kid, M.A.A.D City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Good_Kid,_M.A.A.D_City)*, Lamar's second album and first project under a major record label, was released on October 22, 2012.[^cite_note-72][^cite_note-73] He worked with producers such as [Pharrell Williams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pharrell_Williams), [Hit-Boy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hit-Boy), [Scoop DeVille](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scoop_DeVille), [Jack Splash](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jack_Splash), and [T-Minus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/T-Minus_\(producer\)) to create an atmospheric [West Coast hip-hop](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Coast_hip-hop) album with heavy [gangsta rap](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gangsta_rap) influences.[^cite_note-hiphopdx1-46] Its [lead single](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lead_single), "[Swimming Pools (Drank)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swimming_Pools_\(Drank\))",[^cite_note-74] marked Lamar's first top 20 single on the U.S. [*Billboard* Hot 100](423161.md).[^cite_note-75] Its other singles, "[Backseat Freestyle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Backseat_Freestyle)", "[Poetic Justice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Poetic_Justice_\(song\))", and "[Bitch, Don't Kill My Vibe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bitch,_Don't_Kill_My_Vibe)", enjoyed moderate commercial success.[^cite_note-76][^cite_note-77] *Good Kid, M.A.A.D City* was met with widespread critical acclaim, who lauded Lamar's [nonlinear](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nonlinear_narrative) songwriting and thematic scope. [Greg Kot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greg_Kot) of the *[Chicago Tribune](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chicago_Tribune)* applauded him for giving gangsta [tropes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trope_\(literature\)) a "twist, or sometimes upend\[ing] them completely" on a record that "brims with [comedy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Comedy), complexity and the many voices in \[Lamar's] head."[^cite_note-78] The album debuted at number two on the *Billboard* 200 with 242,000 copies sold;[^cite_note-79] the highest first-week album sales of the year by a male rapper.[^cite_note-80] *Good Kid, M.A.A.D City* surpassed *[The Eminem Show](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Eminem_Show)* (2002) to become the [longest-charting hip-hop studio album](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Billboard_200#Most_weeks_on_the_chart) on the *Billboard* 200.[^cite_note-81] In October 2022, it became the first hip-hop studio album to spend over ten consecutive years on the chart.[^cite_note-82]

From September to October 2012, Lamar headlined the [BET](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BET) Music Matters Tour with Black Hippy and [Stalley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stalley).[^cite_note-83] He won Lyricist of the Year at the [BET Hip Hop Awards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2012_BET_Hip_Hop_Awards),[^cite_note-84] and was featured on [ASAP Rocky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ASAP_Rocky)'s single "[Fuckin' Problems](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fuckin'_Problems)" alongside Drake and [2 Chainz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2_Chainz), which reached the top 10 in the U.S.[^cite_note-85] Lamar embarked on two headlining [concert tours](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Concert_tour) in 2013: a national college tour with [Steve Aoki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steve_Aoki) and his first international tour.[^cite_note-86][^cite_note-87] He struggled with depression, [survivor's guilt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Survivor's_guilt) and [suicidal ideation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suicidal_ideation) during promotional events upon learning of the deaths of three close friends.[^cite_note-88] From October to December 2013, Lamar opened for [Kanye West](523032.md) on his [Yeezus Tour](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Yeezus_Tour), despite disapproval from his label and management team.[^cite_note-89][^cite_note-Gordon-90] He was baptized for a second time during the beginning of the tour, and experienced a [nervous breakdown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nervous_breakdown) near the end.[^cite_note-91][^cite_note-92] Lamar won three awards each during the [BET Awards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BET_Awards_2013) and [BET Hip Hop Awards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2013_BET_Hip_Hop_Awards),[^cite_note-93][^cite_note-94] including [Best New Artist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BET_Award_for_Best_New_Artist) at the former.[^cite_note-95][^cite_note-96]

Lamar was featured on six songs throughout the year: "[YOLO](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/YOLO_\(The_Lonely_Island_song\))" by [the Lonely Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Lonely_Island) featuring [Adam Levine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adam_Levine)*,*[^cite_note-97] the remix of "[How Many Drinks?](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/How_Many_Drinks%3F)" by [Miguel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miguel_\(singer\)),[^cite_note-98] "[Collard Greens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Collard_Greens_\(song\))" by Schoolboy Q,[^cite_note-99] "[Control](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Control_\(Big_Sean_song\))" with [Big Sean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Big_Sean) and [Jay Electronica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jay_Electronica),[^cite_note-100] "[Give It 2 U](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Give_It_2_U)" by [Robin Thicke](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robin_Thicke) featuring 2 Chainz,[^cite_note-101] and "[Love Game](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Marshall_Mathers_LP_2)" by [Eminem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eminem).[^cite_note-102] His performance on "Control" was described as a "wake up call" for the hip-hop industry and commenced [his decade-long feud with Drake](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Drake%E2%80%93Kendrick_Lamar_feud).[^cite_note-103] *Rolling Stone* noted that his verse made the track one of the most important hip-hop songs of the last decade.[^cite_note-104] Lamar was named Rapper of the Year by *[GQ](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/GQ)* during their annual Men of the Year edition.[^cite_note-105] Following the issue's release, Tiffith pulled him from performing at *GQ*'s accompanying party and accused Steve Marsh's profile on him of containing "racial overtones".[^cite_note-106][^cite_note-107]

### 2014–2016: *To Pimp a Butterfly* and *Untitled Unmastered*

After his opening stint for the Yeezus Tour ended, Lamar began work on his third album.[^cite_note-Gordon-90] He earned seven nominations at the [56th Annual Grammy Awards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/56th_Annual_Grammy_Awards) (January 2014), including [Best New Artist](198014.md), [Best Rap Album](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grammy_Award_for_Best_Rap_Album), and [Album of the Year](196513.md) for *Good Kid, M.A.A.D City*.[^cite_note-108] He was winless at the ceremony, which several media outlets felt was a [snub](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Snub).[^cite_note-109][^cite_note-110] [Macklemore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Macklemore), who won Best New Artist and Best Rap Album, shared a text message that he sent Lamar after the ceremony ended, in which he apologized for winning over him.[^cite_note-111] The incident was the subject of widespread media attention, controversy and [Internet memes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Internet_meme).[^cite_note-112] During the awards ceremony, Lamar performed a mashup of "M.A.A.D City" and "[Radioactive](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radioactive_\(Imagine_Dragons_song\))" with [rock](25423.md) band [Imagine Dragons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Imagine_Dragons), which was met with critical acclaim.[^cite_note-113][^cite_note-114]

Lamar opened for [Eminem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eminem) on the [Rapture Tour](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rapture_Tour) from February to July 2014.[^cite_note-115][^cite_note-116] On August 9, he premiered the short film *M.A.A.D*, which he starred in, commissioned and produced, during the [Sundance Institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sundance_Institute)'s Next Fest.[^cite_note-117] He released "[I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/I_\(Kendrick_Lamar_song\))" as the lead single to his second album, *[To Pimp a Butterfly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/To_Pimp_a_Butterfly)*, on September 23, which won [Best Rap Performance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grammy_Award_for_Best_Rap_Performance) and [Best Rap Song](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grammy_Award_for_Best_Rap_Song) at the [57th Annual Grammy Awards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/57th_Annual_Grammy_Awards).[^cite_note-118][^cite_note-119] His performance of "I" during his appearance as a [musical guest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Musical_guest) on *[Saturday Night Live](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saturday_Night_Live)* was lauded by contemporary critics.[^cite_note-120] Lamar was featured on three songs in 2014: "[It's On Again](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/It's_On_Again)" by [Alicia Keys](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alicia_Keys),[^cite_note-121] "[Babylon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Z_\(EP\))" by [SZA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SZA),[^cite_note-122] and "[Never Catch Me](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Never_Catch_Me)" by [Flying Lotus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flying_Lotus).[^cite_note-123] He won Lyricist of the Year for the second consecutive time at the [BET Hip Hop Awards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_BET_Hip_Hop_Awards).[^cite_note-124]



Lamar at the [Hollywood Palladium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hollywood_Palladium) before the [57th Annual Grammy Awards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/57th_Annual_Grammy_Awards) (2015), where he won [Best Rap Performance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grammy_Award_for_Best_Rap_Performance) and [Best Rap Song](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grammy_Award_for_Best_Rap_Song) for "[I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/I_\(Kendrick_Lamar_song\))"

Originally scheduled to arrive at a later date, *To Pimp a Butterfly* was released on March 15, 2015.[^cite_note-125] The album incorporated various genres synonymous with [African American music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African_American_music), such as [jazz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jazz), [funk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Funk), and [soul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soul_music).[^cite_note-126] To capture its essence, Lamar recruited producers such as [Sounwave](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sounwave), [Pharrell Williams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pharrell_Williams), [Terrace Martin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Terrace_Martin), and [Thundercat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thundercat_\(musician\)).[^cite_note-127] Other singles from the album were "[The Blacker the Berry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Blacker_the_Berry_\(song\))",[^cite_note-128] "[King Kunta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/King_Kunta)",[^cite_note-129] "[Alright](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alright_\(Kendrick_Lamar_song\))", and "[These Walls](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/These_Walls_\(Kendrick_Lamar_song\))" – all of which enjoyed moderate commercial success.[^cite_note-130] Selling 324,000 copies in its first week, *To Pimp a Butterfly* became Lamar's first number-one album on the *Billboard* 200 and the [UK Albums Chart](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UK_Albums_Chart).[^cite_note-131][^cite_note-132] *[Billboard](18309966.md)* commented that "twenty years ago, a conscious rap record wouldn't have penetrated the mainstream in the way \[Lamar] did with *To Pimp a Butterfly*. His sense of timing is impeccable. In the midst of rampant cases of police brutality and racial tension across America, he spews raw, aggressive bard while possibly cutting a rug."[^cite_note-Billboard_top_albums_2015-133] *[Pitchfork](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pitchfork_\(website\))* opined that the album "forced critics to think deeply about music."[^cite_note-Ex-134]

He earned his first number-one single in the U.S. through the remix of singer-songwriter [Taylor Swift](5422144.md)'s "[Bad Blood](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bad_Blood_\(Taylor_Swift_song\))".[^cite_note-135][^cite_note-136] It won [Video of the Year](476903.md) and [Best Collaboration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MTV_Video_Music_Award_for_Best_Collaboration) at the [MTV Video Music Awards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2015_MTV_Video_Music_Awards), while the music video for "Alright" won [Best Direction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MTV_Video_Music_Award_for_Best_Direction).[^cite_note-137] Lamar later [re-recorded](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Re-recording_\(music\)) his featured appearance on the "[Bad Blood](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bad_Blood_\(Taylor_Swift_song\)#%22Bad_Blood_\(Taylor's_Version\)%22)" remix in support of Swift's counteraction to [her masters dispute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taylor_Swift_masters_dispute).[^cite_note-138][^cite_note-139] He opened the [BET Awards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BET_Awards_2015) with a controversial performance of "Alright" and won Best Male Hip Hop Artist.[^cite_note-140] He also won three awards at the [BET Hip Hop Awards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2015_BET_Hip_Hop_Awards).[^cite_note-141] In support of *To Pimp a Butterfly*, Lamar embarked on the Kunta's Groove Sessions Tour, which ran from October to November 2015 in select intimate venues across the U.S.[^cite_note-142] For his work on the album and other collaborations throughout the year, Lamar earned 11 nominations at the [58th Annual Grammy Awards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/58th_Annual_Grammy_Awards), [the most by a rapper in a single night](16116273.md).[^cite_note-grammys16noms-143] He led the winners with five awards: *To Pimp a Butterfly* was named Best Rap Album, "Alright" won Best Rap Performance and Best Rap Song, "These Walls" won [Best Rap/Sung Performance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grammy_Award_for_Best_Melodic_Rap_Performance), and "Bad Blood" won [Best Music Video](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grammy_Award_for_Best_Music_Video).[^cite_note-grammys16-144]

During the ceremony, Lamar performed a critically acclaimed medley of "The Blacker the Berry", "Alright", and [an untitled song](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Untitled_03_%E2%80%93_05.28.2013.).[^cite_note-auto-145] He previously performed untitled songs on *[The Colbert Report](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Colbert_Report)* (December 2014) and *[The Tonight Show Starring Jimmy Fallon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Tonight_Show_Starring_Jimmy_Fallon)* (January 2016).[^cite_note-146][^cite_note-147] After receiving a request from basketball player [LeBron James](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LeBron_James) to share the untitled works, Lamar released his first compilation album, *[Untitled Unmastered](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Untitled_Unmastered)*, on March 4, 2016.[^cite_note-148] It contained eight untitled, dated, unfinished, and entirely self-written tracks that were intended to be included on *To Pimp a Butterfly*, and continued the album's exploration of jazz, funk, soul, and [avant-garde](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Avant-garde_music) styles.[^cite_note-149] *Untitled Unmastered* received critical acclaim and debuted atop the *Billboard* 200 with 178,000 [album-equivalent units](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Album-equivalent_unit), becoming Lamar's second consecutive number-one project.[^cite_note-150] Throughout the year, he was featured on four commercially successful songs: [Beyoncé](83688.md)'s "[Freedom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedom_\(Beyonc%C3%A9_song\))",[^cite_note-151] [Maroon 5](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maroon_5)'s "[Don't Wanna Know](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Don't_Wanna_Know)",[^cite_note-152][^cite_note-153] [the Weeknd](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Weeknd)'s "[Sidewalks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sidewalks_\(The_Weeknd_song\))",[^cite_note-154] and [Travis Scott](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Travis_Scott)'s "[Goosebumps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Goosebumps_\(Travis_Scott_song\))".[^cite_note-155]

### 2017–2019: *Damn* and *Black Panther: The Album*



Lamar performing on [The Damn Tour](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Damn_Tour) at [TD Garden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/TD_Garden) in July 2017

On March 1, 2017, during a cover story for *[T](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/T_\(magazine\))*, Lamar confirmed that he was working on his third album, *[Damn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Damn_\(album\))*.[^cite_note-Mason-156] He released the promotional single "[The Heart Part 4](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Heart_Part_4)" on March 23, before releasing the album's lead single "[Humble](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Humble_\(song\))" on March 30.[^cite_note-157][^cite_note-158] The song debuted at number two on the Hot 100 and reached the top spot in its second week of charting. It is Lamar's second single, and first as a lead artist, to top the chart.[^cite_note-159] *Damn* was released on April 14.[^cite_note-160] It utilized a more mainstream musical palette than *To Pimp a Butterfly*, exploring [R\&B](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Contemporary_R\&B) and [pop](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pop_music) elements.[^cite_note-RSDAMN-161] *Rolling Stone* described its sonics as a "brilliant combination of the timeless and the modern, the old school and the next-level."[^cite_note-162] *Damn* became Lamar's most commercially successful album. It spent four non-consecutive weeks atop the *Billboard* 200, marking his third consecutive number-one album, and debuted with 603,000 units sold.[^cite_note-163][^cite_note-164] All of the album's 14 songs debuted on the Hot 100, including the top-20 singles "[Loyalty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Loyalty_\(Kendrick_Lamar_song\))" and "[Love](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Love_\(Kendrick_Lamar_song\))".[^cite_note-165] *Damn* was the seventh best-selling album of 2017, according to the [International Federation of the Phonographic Industry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Federation_of_the_Phonographic_Industry) (IFPI), while "Humble" was the sixth best-selling single of the year.[^cite_note-166] By June 2018, it became the first album by a rapper or solo artist to have every song featured earn a [gold certification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/RIAA_certification) or higher from the [Recording Industry Association of America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Recording_Industry_Association_of_America).[^cite_note-167]

To support *Damn*, Lamar embarked on his first headlining arena tour, [the Damn Tour](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Damn_Tour), from July 2017 to July 2018.[^cite_note-168] It grossed \$62.7 million in worldwide revenue, becoming one of the highest-grossing hip-hop tours in history.[^cite_note-169] At the [2017 MTV Video Music Awards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2017_MTV_Video_Music_Awards), Lamar opened the ceremony with a performance of "DNA" and "Humble".[^cite_note-170] He later won five awards, including [Best Hip Hop Video](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MTV_Video_Music_Award_for_Best_Hip_Hop_Video), Best Direction, and Video of the Year for "Humble"; the latter win marked the first time an artist won the prize for a video they co-directed.[^cite_note-171][^cite_note-172] Throughout the year, he was featured on the remix to [Future](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Future_\(rapper\))'s "[Mask Off](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mask_Off)",[^cite_note-173] [SZA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SZA)'s "[Doves in the Wind](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doves_in_the_Wind)",[^cite_note-174][^cite_note-175] and [Rich the Kid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rich_the_Kid)'s "[New Freezer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Freezer)".[^cite_note-176] He won Best Male Hip Hop Artist at the [BET Awards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BET_Awards_2017),[^cite_note-177] while *Damn* won [Favorite Rap/Hip Hop Album](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Music_Award_for_Favorite_Rap/Hip_Hop_Album) at the [American Music Awards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Music_Awards_of_2017).[^cite_note-178] A [collector's edition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Collector's_edition) of the album, which featured its tracklist in reverse order, was released in December.[^cite_note-179][^cite_note-180]

On January 4, 2018, Lamar announced that he would be [curating](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Curator) and [executive producing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Executive_producer) *[Black Panther: The Album](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_Panther_\(soundtrack\))*, the [soundtrack](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soundtrack_album) from [the 2018 film](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_Panther_\(film\)).[^cite_note-Black_Panther_Soundtrack-181] It was released on February 9 and was supported with three commercially successful singles: "[All the Stars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/All_the_Stars)",[^cite_note-182] "[King's Dead](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/King's_Dead)",[^cite_note-183] and "[Pray for Me](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pray_for_Me_\(The_Weeknd_and_Kendrick_Lamar_song\))".[^cite_note-184][^cite_note-185] Lamar contributed lead and background vocals to every track on the album, regardless of credit, and produced on select songs.[^cite_note-186][^cite_note-187] Music critics consider *Black Panther: The Album* to be a milestone achievement, giving praise towards its lyrics and cultural significance.[^cite_note-188][^cite_note-189] It spent two consecutive weeks atop the *Billboard* 200,[^cite_note-190] and earned the most single-week streams for a soundtrack album in history.[^cite_note-191] Lamar opened the [60th Annual Grammy Awards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/60th_Annual_Grammy_Awards) with a critically acclaimed [medley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medley_\(music\)).[^cite_note-192] He won five awards during the ceremony: *Damn* was named Best Rap Album, "Humble" won Best Rap Performance, Best Rap Song, and Best Music Video and "Loyalty" won Best Rap/Sung Performance.[^cite_note-damn_grammys-193] *Damn* won the [Pulitzer Prize for Music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pulitzer_Prize_for_Music) on April 16, 2018, marking the first time a musical composition outside of the classical and jazz genres received the honor.[^cite_note-194][^cite_note-195]

From May to June 2018, Lamar co-headlined [the Championship Tour](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Championship_Tour) with several TDE artists.[^cite_note-196][^cite_note-197] While on tour, he became embroiled in a public dispute with Spotify regarding the streaming service's [Hate Content & Hateful Conduct](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Criticism_of_Spotify#2017%E2%80%9318:_Hate_Content_&_Hateful_Conduct_policy) policy.[^cite_note-TD-198][^cite_note-199] Lamar was featured on five songs throughout the year: "[Dedication](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dedication_\(Nipsey_Hussle_song\))" by [Nipsey Hussle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nipsey_Hussle),[^cite_note-200][^cite_note-201] "[Mona Lisa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mona_Lisa_\(Lil_Wayne_song\))" by Lil Wayne,[^cite_note-202] "[Tints](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tints_\(song\))" by [Anderson .Paak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anderson_.Paak),[^cite_note-203][^cite_note-204] and "Wow Freestyle" by Jay Rock; he also executive produced the latter's album *[Redemption](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Redemption_\(Jay_Rock_album\))* and provided background vocals for the album's second single "[Win](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Win_\(song\))".[^cite_note-205][^cite_note-206] At the [American Music Awards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Music_Awards_of_2018), *Black Panther: The Album* won Favorite Rap/Hip-Hop Album.[^cite_note-207] Lamar made his acting debut as a drug addict in the [crime](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crime_fiction) [drama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Drama_\(film_and_television\)) series *[Power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Power_\(TV_series\))* (2018).[^cite_note-208] After his two concert tours ended, he entered a four-year recording hiatus;[^cite_note-209] although he contributed to Beyoncé's *[The Lion King: The Gift](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Lion_King:_The_Gift)*, Schoolboy Q's *[Crash Talk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crash_Talk)*, and [Sir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sir_\(singer\))'s *[Chasing Summer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chasing_Summer)* (all 2019).[^cite_note-210][^cite_note-211] As his publishing deal with Warner/Chappell Music was beginning to expire,[^cite_note-\:0-43] Lamar signed a long-term worldwide deal with [Broadcast Music, Inc.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Broadcast_Music,_Inc.)[^cite_note-212]

### 2020–2023: *Mr. Morale & the Big Steppers*

On March 5, 2020, Lamar and Dave Free launched the creative entity [PGLang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PGLang), which was described at the time as a multilingual, artist-friendly service company.[^cite_note-213][^cite_note-214] In October, he signed a worldwide administration agreement with [Universal Music Publishing Group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Universal_Music_Publishing_Group).[^cite_note-215] Lamar announced through an August 2021 [blog](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blog) post that he was in the process of producing his final album under TDE, confirming rumors that emerged the year before that he would be leaving to focus on PGLang.[^cite_note-216][^cite_note-217] The following week, he appeared on [Baby Keem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baby_Keem)'s single "[Family Ties](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Family_Ties_\(song\))", which won Best Rap Performance at the [64th Annual Grammy Awards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/64th_Annual_Grammy_Awards).[^cite_note-218][^cite_note-219] Lamar made additional contributions to Keem's album *[The Melodic Blue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Melodic_Blue)* by providing background vocals and appearing on the song "[Range Brothers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Range_Brothers)".[^cite_note-220] In November, he held a "theatrical exhibition of his musical eras" during his second headlining performance at Day N Vegas,[^cite_note-221][^cite_note-222] and featured on Terrace Martin's album *Drones*.[^cite_note-223] In January 2022, he signed on to produce [a comedy feature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Untitled_Trey_Parker_film) with Free, [Trey Parker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trey_Parker) and [Matt Stone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Matt_Stone) for [Paramount Pictures](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paramount_Pictures).[^cite_note-224][^cite_note-225][^cite_note-226] The film was originally slated for a July 2025 release but has been delayed twice with no official release date planned as of September 2026.[^cite_note-Gajewski2026-227] He co-headlined the [Super Bowl LVI halftime show](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Super_Bowl_LVI_halftime_show) alongside Dr. Dre, Snoop Dogg, Eminem, [50 Cent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/50_Cent), and [Mary J. Blige](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mary_J._Blige) on February 13, 2022, which won the [Primetime Emmy Award for Outstanding Variety Special (Live)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Primetime_Emmy_Award_for_Outstanding_Variety_Special_\(Live\)).[^cite_note-228][^cite_note-229]



Lamar performing at the [Spectrum Center](417260.md) for [The Big Steppers Tour](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Big_Steppers_Tour) in 2022

After releasing the promotional single "[The Heart Part 5](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Heart_Part_5)",[^cite_note-230][^cite_note-231] Lamar's fifth album, *[Mr. Morale & the Big Steppers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mr._Morale_&_the_Big_Steppers)*, was released on May 13, 2022.[^cite_note-Sisario-2022-232] The [double album](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Double_album) drew on jazz, R\&B, [trap](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trap_music), and soul influences.[^cite_note-233][^cite_note-Amorosi-2022-234] It was widely acclaimed by critics, who applauded Lamar's vulnerable songwriting and scope.[^cite_note-235] Every track from the album charted on the Hot 100; its three singles – "[N95](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/N95_\(song\))", "[Silent Hill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Silent_Hill_\(song\))", and "[Die Hard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Die_Hard_\(song\))" – debuted in the top-10.[^cite_note-236] Selling 295,000 units in its first week, *Mr. Morale & the Big Steppers* became Lamar's fourth consecutive number-one album on the *Billboard* 200.[^cite_note-237] It later became the first hip-hop album of the year to reach one billion streams on Spotify.[^cite_note-238]

In support of *Mr. Morale & the Big Steppers*, Lamar embarked on [the Big Steppers Tour](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Big_Steppers_Tour), which ran from July 2022 to March 2024.[^cite_note-239] The tour grossed \$110.9 million in worldwide revenue, becoming the highest-grossing rap tour ever at the time.[^cite_note-240] Lamar wrote, co-directed, and executive produced the short [film adaptation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Film_adaptation) of the song "[We Cry Together](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/We_Cry_Together)", which was released worldwide in September 2022.[^cite_note-241] An accompanying [concert film](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Concert_film) for the tour, *[Kendrick Lamar Live: The Big Steppers Tour](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kendrick_Lamar_Live:_The_Big_Steppers_Tour)*, was released in November.[^cite_note-242][^cite_note-243] Lamar won [Favorite Male Hip Hop Artist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Music_Award_for_Favorite_Rap/Hip_Hop_Artist) at the [American Music Awards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Music_Awards_of_2022), and Favorite Hip Hop Album for *Mr. Morale & the Big Steppers*. He received six awards at the [BET Hip Hop Awards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_BET_Hip_Hop_Awards), including [Album of the Year](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BET_Hip_Hop_Award_for_Album_of_the_Year).[^cite_note-244][^cite_note-245] During the [65th Annual Grammy Awards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/65th_Annual_Grammy_Awards), *Mr. Morale & the Big Steppers* was named Best Rap Album, while "The Heart Part 5" won Best Rap Performance and Best Rap Song.[^cite_note-246]

In May 2023, Lamar was featured on the standalone version of [Beyoncé](83688.md)'s single "[America Has a Problem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/America_Has_a_Problem)" and appeared on Baby Keem's single "[The Hillbillies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Hillbillies)".[^cite_note-247][^cite_note-248] He won four awards at the [BET Hip Hop Awards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_BET_Hip_Hop_Awards), and set four records in the process.[^cite_note-249] Lamar was featured in the [documentary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Documentary) concert film *[Renaissance: A Film by Beyoncé](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Renaissance:_A_Film_by_Beyonc%C3%A9)* and executive produced [Baby Keem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baby_Keem)'s short film adaptation of *The Melodic Blue*.[^cite_note-250][^cite_note-251] He quietly shed his ties with Aftermath Entertainment and signed a new direct licensing agreement with Interscope.[^cite_note-252]

### 2024–present: Drake feud, *GNX*, Super Bowl LIX halftime show and Grand National Tour

Further information: [Drake–Kendrick Lamar feud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Drake%E2%80%93Kendrick_Lamar_feud)



Lamar at the [Tottenham Hotspur Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tottenham_Hotspur_Stadium) during his [Grand National Tour](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grand_National_Tour) in 2025

Lamar's conflict with Drake [re-escalated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Drake%E2%80%93Kendrick_Lamar_feud#2023%E2%80%9324:_Direct_confrontation) in March 2024 with his surprise appearance on [Future](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Future_\(rapper\)) and [Metro Boomin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metro_Boomin)'s track "[Like That](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Like_That_\(Future,_Metro_Boomin_and_Kendrick_Lamar_song\))".[^cite_note-253] The song spent three consecutive weeks atop the *Billboard* Hot 100, becoming Lamar's third number-one single and his first song to debut at the top spot.[^cite_note-254] From April to May, he released the Drake-aimed diss singles "[Euphoria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Euphoria_\(Kendrick_Lamar_song\))",[^cite_note-255] "[6\:16 in LA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/6\:16_in_LA)",[^cite_note-256] "[Meet the Grahams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Meet_the_Grahams)",[^cite_note-257] and "[Not Like Us](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Not_Like_Us)"; all of which were either positively received or acclaimed by critics.[^cite_note-258] The latter installment marked the first rap song to lead the Hot 100 with a limited tracking week.[^cite_note-259] A celebratory one-off concert, titled [The Pop Out: Ken & Friends](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Pop_Out:_Ken_&_Friends), was held on [Juneteenth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juneteenth).[^cite_note-260] "Not Like Us" went on to win five awards at the [67th Annual Grammy Awards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/67th_Annual_Grammy_Awards) on February 2, 2025: [Record of the Year](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grammy_Award_for_Record_of_the_Year), [Song of the Year](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grammy_Award_for_Song_of_the_Year), [Best Rap Performance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grammy_Award_for_Best_Rap_Performance), [Best Rap Song](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grammy_Award_for_Best_Rap_Song), and [Best Music Video](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grammy_Award_for_Best_Music_Video).[^cite_note-261]

Lamar released "[Watch the Party Die](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Watch_the_Party_Die)" to his Instagram account in September.[^cite_note-262] On November 22, he shared a song titled "GNX", exclusively on [YouTube](3524766.md), followed with a surprise release of the [album of the same name](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/GNX_\(album\)) on streaming the same day.[^cite_note-263][^cite_note-264] The record spawned a series of successful singles, including the *Billboard* Hot 100 number-one singles "[Squabble Up](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Squabble_Up)" and "[Luther](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luther_\(Kendrick_Lamar_song\))". "Squabble Up" debuted at the top of the chart,[^cite_note-d597-265] while "Luther", which featured [SZA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SZA), became his longest charting number-one song in the US, spending thirteen non-consecutive weeks atop the Hot 100.[^cite_note-p071-266] On December 17, he released a song called "[Money Without Me](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Money_Without_Me)" on YouTube, rumored to be from *[Section.80](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Section.80)* sessions. That month, he had a guest appearance on SZA's *[Lana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lana_\(album\))*, the deluxe reissue to *[SOS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SOS_\(SZA_album\))* (2022). *Billboard* listed Lamar as the [greatest pop star of 2024](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Billboard's_Greatest_Pop_Stars#By_year), highlighting his "new releases or revelations that captured headlines and captivated the culture".[^cite_note-267] Outside of music, Lamar starred in the animated [biographical film](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biographical_film) *[Piece by Piece](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Piece_by_Piece_\(2024_film\))* (2024).[^cite_note-268]

On February 9, 2025, Lamar headlined the [Super Bowl LIX halftime show](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Super_Bowl_LIX_halftime_show), which featured performances from SZA, [Samuel L. Jackson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samuel_L._Jackson), [Serena Williams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Serena_Williams), and [Mustard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mustard_\(record_producer\)).[^cite_note-269][^cite_note-270][^cite_note-271] It became the most-watched Super Bowl performance of all time with 133.5 million viewers, surpassing Michael Jackson's 1993 [Super Bowl XXVII halftime show](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Super_Bowl_XXVII_halftime_show).[^cite_note-272] For his work on the event, Lamar won the [Primetime Emmy Award for Outstanding Music Direction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Primetime_Emmy_Award_for_Outstanding_Music_Direction).[^cite_note-273] On February 17, he became the first rapper (and ninth artist overall) to accumulate over 100 million monthly listeners on Spotify.[^cite_note-274] He further became the first rapper to have three top-10 albums in the US simultaneously.[^cite_note-275] On March 14, 2025, Lamar was featured thrice on the album *[Music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Music_\(Playboi_Carti_album\))* by [Playboi Carti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Playboi_Carti), appearing on the tracks "[Mojo Jojo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mojo_Jojo_\(song\))", "[Backd00r](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Backd00r)", and "[Good Credit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Good_Credit)".[^cite_note-276] Alongside rock musician [Lenny Kravitz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lenny_Kravitz) and frequent collaborator [Pharrell Williams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pharrell_Williams), Lamar was featured on [Clipse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clipse)'s song "[Chains & Whips](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chains_&_Whips)" from the album *[Let God Sort Em Out](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Let_God_Sort_Em_Out),* for which he and the duo would win the award for Best Rap Performance at the [68th Annual Grammy Awards](79128292.md).[^cite_note-277][^cite_note-278] In support of *GNX* and *Lana*, Lamar and SZA embarked on the [Grand National Tour](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grand_National_Tour) starting in April 2025,[^cite_note-279] breaking the record for the highest-grossing co-headlining tour.[^cite_note-280] Lamar featured on Baby Keem's album [*Casino*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Casino_\(Baby_Keem_album\)) on the track "[Good Flirts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Good_Flirts)" with [Momo Boyd](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Infinity_Song) in February 2026.[^cite_note-281]

## Artistry

### Influences

Headshot of Tupac Shakur on a white background

Lamar considers [Tupac Shakur](31341.md) to be his biggest influence.

[Tupac Shakur](31341.md) is Lamar's biggest influence, having impacted both his professional and personal life.[^cite_note-282] One of his earliest childhood memories is watching him and [Dr. Dre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dr._Dre) film the second music video for their single "[California Love](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/California_Love)" with his father at the Compton [Swap Meet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flea_market).[^cite_note-Greenburg-13] Lamar has described himself as an "offspring" of Shakur's artistry and [sociopolitical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_sociology) views.[^cite_note-283] Although some publications have regarded him as the Shakur of his generation,[^cite_note-284][^cite_note-285] he has strived to maintain his [individuality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Individual).[^cite_note-286]

Shakur's *[The Don Killuminati: The 7 Day Theory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Don_Killuminati:_The_7_Day_Theory)* (1996), [the Notorious B.I.G.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Notorious_B.I.G.)'s *[Life After Death](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Life_After_Death)* (1997), and [DMX](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/DMX)'s *[It's Dark and Hell Is Hot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/It's_Dark_and_Hell_Is_Hot)* (1998) influenced Lamar's artistic direction: "I don't look at these albums like just music; it sounds like an actual film."[^cite_note-287] He also listened and took influence from [Mos Def](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mos_Def) and [Snoop Dogg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Snoop_Dogg) during his childhood,[^cite_note-288] and said, "I wouldn't be here today if it wasn't for [Eazy-E](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eazy-E)."[^cite_note-289] [50 Cent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/50_Cent)'s mixtape success inspired Lamar to become an [independent artist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Independent_music),[^cite_note-Hopper-8] while his view on being categorized as a [conscious rapper](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conscious_rap), "Yeah, I'm a conscious artist because I have a [conscience](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conscience)", gave him a sense of perspective.[^cite_note-290]

[Prodigy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prodigy_\(rapper\)) of [Mobb Deep](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mobb_Deep) was a key influence on Lamar's earlier mixtapes,[^cite_note-291] while his rapping technique was stemmed from [Lil Wayne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lil_Wayne) and his longevity.[^cite_note-292] [Eminem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eminem) and his 2000 album *[The Marshall Mathers LP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Marshall_Mathers_LP)* introduced him to songwriting elements, such as [ad-libs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ad_libitum), and impacted his aggressive approach to records such as "[Backseat Freestyle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Backseat_Freestyle)".[^cite_note-293][^cite_note-294][^cite_note-295] He took inspiration from [N.W.A](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/N.W.A)'s tenacity of representing his hometown with "courage, honesty and artistic brilliance",[^cite_note-296] and credited [Kanye West](523032.md) with teaching him to "never have any boundaries".[^cite_note-297] Various [R\&B](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rhythm_and_blues) and [soul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soul_music) artists, including [Marvin Gaye](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marvin_Gaye),[^cite_note-Ryon12-298] [the Isley Brothers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Isley_Brothers),[^cite_note-Ryon12-298] [Michael Jackson](14995351.md),[^cite_note-299] [Teddy Pendergrass](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Teddy_Pendergrass),[^cite_note-300] [Sade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sade_\(singer\)), and [Anita Baker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anita_Baker), have influenced Lamar.[^cite_note-301] He performed with [Prince](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prince_\(musician\)), who impacted his [vocal register](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vocal_register),[^cite_note-302] at [Paisley Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paisley_Park) to celebrate the release of the latter's 2014 albums *[Plectrumelectrum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plectrumelectrum)* and *[Art Official Age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Art_Official_Age)*, which *[GQ](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/GQ)* described as "five minutes of brilliant insanity".[^cite_note-303] *To Pimp a Butterfly* was influenced by the works of [jazz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jazz) trumpeter [Miles Davis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miles_Davis) and [funk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Funk) collective [Parliament-Funkadelic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliament-Funkadelic).[^cite_note-304]

### Musical style

The nature of Lamar's musical style has been described as "anti-flamboyant, interior and complex."[^cite_note-NYT_Review-305] He is rooted in [West Coast hip hop](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Coast_hip_hop),[^cite_note-306] and has continually reinvented his sound by branching out into other genres.[^cite_note-Timeline-307] Due to his contributions to its audience growth, through his appeal to [mainstream](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mainstream_media) listeners, music critics generally categorize Lamar as a [progressive rap](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Progressive_rap) artist.[^cite_note-308] He suggests that his music is genreless, explaining in a 2012 interview, "You really can't categorize my music, it's human music."[^cite_note-309] *PopDust* opined that during the 2010s, a decade that was arguably defined by hip hop, Lamar constantly pushed the boundaries of what the genre could be.[^cite_note-310]

Lamar did not care for [music production](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Record_producer) during the beginning of his career.[^cite_note-Barker-311] However, as he placed an emphasis on songwriting and "making material that's universal", he grew more exacting and adventurous with his [compositions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Musical_composition).[^cite_note-Barker-311] He is heavily involved with every aspect of his production process, including the [mixing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Audio_mixing_\(recorded_music\)) and [mastering](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mastering_\(audio\)) stages, and is known for working long hours in the recording studio.[^cite_note-312] "You gotta be hands on and know the different sounds and [frequencies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Audio_frequency)," Lamar explained to *[Variety](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Variety_\(magazine\))*.[^cite_note-Barker-311] "What makes people move, what [melodies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Melody) stick with you, taking the higher [octaves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Octave) and the lower octaves and learning how to intertwine that in a certain frequency, how to manipulate sound to your advantage."[^cite_note-Barker-311] Lamar chooses to work with a close-knit team of musicians, rather than constantly seek high-profile talent.[^cite_note-hiphopdx1-46] He has been working with his longtime producer, [Sounwave](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sounwave), since his 2009 self-titled EP.[^cite_note-Mixtapes-29]

*Kendrick Lamar* marked a pivotal change in Lamar's artistry. Unlike his earlier mixtapes, which consisted of freestyles over [CHR](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Contemporary_hit_radio) and [urban radio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urban_contemporary_music) singles, the EP incorporated melancholic and "doleful" original production that emphasized his lyrics.[^cite_note-Mixtapes-29] Austere jazz production was blended with [alternative rap](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alternative_hip_hop) styles on *Section.80*,[^cite_note-Breihan-2021-56] with instrumentals drawing from R\&B, [boom bap](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boom_bap), [psychedelia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Psychedelic_music), and [downtempo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Downtempo).[^cite_note-313] *Good Kid, M.A.A.D City* abandoned the tastes of contemporary hip hop by exploring a subtle, atmospheric side of West Coast hip hop and [gangsta rap](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gangsta_rap).[^cite_note-Caramanica-314][^cite_note-Madden-315] *To Pimp a Butterfly* is an amalgamation of genres synonymous with [African-American music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African-American_music), most prominently jazz, [funk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Funk), and soul.[^cite_note-Anderson-316][^cite_note-oral_history-317] It redefined [jazz rap](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jazz_rap) by highlighting [improvisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jazz_improvisation) and [soloing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solo_\(music\)) rather than primarily using [sampling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sampling_\(music\)).[^cite_note-318][^cite_note-319] [Minimalist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minimal_music) [arrangements](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arrangement) are incorporated in *Damn* and *Mr. Morale & the Big Steppers.*[^cite_note-320][^cite_note-Amorosi-2022-234] *Damn* appealed to mainstream listeners through its [pop](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pop_music) and R\&B-influenced production,[^cite_note-321] while the scattered and [distorted](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Distortion_\(music\)) instrumentals of *Mr. Morale & the Big Steppers* was designed to make listeners feel anxious and uncomfortable.[^cite_note-322] *GNX* was a tribute to Lamar's native Los Angeles and prominently infused [G-funk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/G-funk) and [regional Mexican](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Regional_Mexican) compositions.[^cite_note-323]

### Voice

Several media outlets consider Lamar to be the greatest and most important rapper of his generation.[^cite_note-324][^cite_note-325] *Billboard*, *[Forbes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forbes)* and *[Vibe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vibe_\(magazine\))* named him the [second-greatest rapper of all time](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Billboard_and_Vibe's_50_Greatest_Rappers_of_All_Time), behind [Jay-Z](162870.md).[^cite_note-326][^cite_note-327] Described as a "blazing" technical rapper and "relentless searcher" by *[The New York Times](30680.md)*,[^cite_note-NYT_Review-305] Lamar's "limber, dexterous" flow switches from [derivative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Morphological_derivation) to [generative metrics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Generative_metrics),[^cite_note-328][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMattessich20191–12-329] while incorporating [internal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Internal_rhyme) and [multisyllabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multisyllabic_rhymes) [rhyme schemes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rhyme_scheme).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWadsworthNeedle202269–94-330] His rhymes are typically manipulated within [common time](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Time_signature), allowing him to subtly control his [metrical phonology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metrical_phonology) and suggest formal [ambiguities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ambiguity) similar to pop and [rock](25423.md) repertoires.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWadsworthNeedle202269–94-330] Some of his rhyme manipulations feature "flexible" [new school](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New-school_hip_hop) styles evoking the 1990s, while others use "rigid" [old school](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old-school_hip_hop) elements recalling the 1980s.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWadsworthNeedle202269–94-330] Lamar frequently uses [syncopation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syncopation) in his melodies to create contradictions between his lyrical content and rhythms.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESloanHardingGottlieb201987–C9.F1-331] With *Good Kid, M.A.A.D City*, he liberally plays with [pronunciation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pronunciation), [inflections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inflection), and delivery to mirror the album's emotional range.[^cite_note-332]

Lamar possesses a versatile [tenor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tenor) [vocal range](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vocal_range)[^cite_note-333][^cite_note-334] and a raspy, half-shout [timbre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timbre), where "his throat sounds dry and his mouth sounds wet."[^cite_note-335] [André 3000](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andr%C3%A9_3000) was the first rapper that introduced him to singing sensibilities in hip hop, and he writes melody-driven songs as practice for his albums.[^cite_note-Hiatt-17] Lamar became comfortable with his vocals over time, to the point where he feels confident enough to create singing-based albums.[^cite_note-336] *Pitchfork* noticed how his [harmonies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vocal_harmony) on *To Pimp a Butterfly* never made him sound alone throughout his "desolate" performance; comparing his [vocal layering](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Double_tracking) to "standing in the middle, unnoticed, of a large quarrelsome crowd."[^cite_note-337]

Praised for his willingness to use his voice as an instrument,[^cite_note-338] Lamar adopts different [cadences](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cadence), [tones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tone_\(linguistics\)), [modulations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modulation_\(music\)), and timbres to suggest conflicting personalities, paint distinct emotions, and communicate stories using [characters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Character_\(arts\)) and [personas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persona).[^cite_note-339][^cite_note-Félix-340] His [falsetto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falsetto) register, which he calls the "ghetto falsetto",[^cite_note-Hiatt-17] has been likened to [Curtis Mayfield](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Curtis_Mayfield)'s.[^cite_note-341] [MTV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MTV) writes that by manipulating his voice, Lamar calls back to a lineage that runs through [James Brown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Brown)'s foundational work in the 1960s, 1970s psychedelia, Prince's "sweaty" [phantasmagoria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phantasmagoria) in the 1980s, and 1990s [gangsta rap](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gangsta_rap).[^cite_note-342] He was ranked the tenth-best solo singer of the 21st century by *[The Times](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Times)* in 2023.[^cite_note-343]

### Songwriting

Further information: [List of songs recorded by Kendrick Lamar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_songs_recorded_by_Kendrick_Lamar)

Martin Scorsese at the 60th Berlinale in 2010

Quentin Tarantino in Paris in 2013

Several publications have compared Lamar's songwriting to [Martin Scorsese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Martin_Scorsese) (left) and [Quentin Tarantino](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quentin_Tarantino)'s (right) screenplays.

Branded as a "master of [storytelling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Storytelling)" by *[The New Yorker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_New_Yorker)*,[^cite_note-Félix-340] Lamar has been referred to as one of the greatest [lyricists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lyricist) in modern hip hop by several publications and his peers.[^cite_note-344][^cite_note-Shifferaw-2022-345] [Pharrell Williams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pharrell_Williams) suggests that what makes his songwriting stand out is because he "knows how to be very disciplined with a subject matter, he knows that stickiness is important, and he knows that it has to be great."[^cite_note-346] *[American Songwriter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Songwriter)* notes that for as much as Lamar is a musician, lyricist, and emcee, he is also "a [playwright](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Playwright), a [novelist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Novelist), a [short story](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Short_story) author. He's [literary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Literature) within the art form of music."[^cite_note-347] Lamar's [reflective](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reflective_writing) narrative songwriting pulls from a wide range of [literary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_narrative_techniques) and [cinematic techniques](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cinematic_techniques), such as [hip hop skits](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hip_hop_skit) and [voice-overs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Voice-over), to allow his audience to follow internal and external [storylines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plot_\(narrative\)).[^cite_note-Hellerbach-348] His fusion of various [film styles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Film_styles) and his sonic influence has elevated his works to be some of the most "consistently poignant" in hip hop, and promoted the advancement of the [narrative device](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plot_device).[^cite_note-Hellerbach-348]

Lamar, who self-identifies as a musician and writer,[^cite_note-Mason-156] begins his songwriting process with an assortment of premeditated thoughts that he jotted down over the course of one year.[^cite_note-GQ16-349] His personal experiences are a common source of inspiration, but he also pulls ideas from meeting new people, traveling, and experiencing different [cultures](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture).[^cite_note-GQ16-349] A devout [Christian](18337522.md), he additionally shares his [spiritual](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spirituality) triumphs and struggles on his songs.[^cite_note-350][^cite_note-351] He is an avid [note-taker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Note-taking), and has developed keywords,
